I don’t think it’s caused any major problems with my friends or family, at least not that I’ve noticed. I might be a little more self-conscious around them sometimes, but I can’t say it’s really affected the relationships.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would stop worrying about your face if this one uneven-looking part were fixed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d stop completely. It can take up enough time that I fall behind on planning things.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or urges that you feel compelled to act on, unrelated to your appearance?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not like something terrible would happen, exactly. It’s more that I get really anxious and stuck on it if I don’t check.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because, since I’ve had to be in front of people more for work, I’ve been really stuck on this one part of my face that looks uneven to me. Especially in photos, I can’t stop looking at it. Other people say they barely notice anything, but I keep seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Does this concern about your face affect other parts of your life beyond just being in front of people or looking at photos?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. It’s started affecting my work, not just photos. I actually missed part of a planning deadline because I spent so much time worrying about how I looked in the event materials. And now things like vendor calls and meeting prep take me way longer than they should, because I get stuck on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself performing any specific rituals or behaviors in an attempt to alleviate your concern about your face?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before I send event pictures or get on a vendor meeting, I’ll check my face in my phone camera over and over. Sometimes I retake pictures a bunch of times or tilt my head a certain way so it looks less uneven. I’ll also ask a coworker if I look weird. It’s embarrassing, but it can take up enough time that I fall behind on planning work.\n\nDoctor: Is the part of your face that you're concerned about something that you think is actually malformed or abnormal in some way?\n\nPatient: I mean, to me it does look abnormal, like one part of my face is uneven. But I know other people say they barely notice it, so I’m not sure if it’s actually malformed or if I’m just seeing it that way. I just can’t stop focusing on it, especially in photos.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern limited to this one part of your face that you mentioned looks uneven to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s mostly that one uneven-looking part of my face. Since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, I just keep noticing it in photos and staring at it, even though other people tell me they barely see anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would look perfect if this one part were fixed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say perfect. It’s more like… ever since I’ve had to be in front of people more at work, I can’t stop focusing on that one uneven-looking part in photos. Even when everyone else says they barely notice anything, it still really bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think it’s caused any major problems with my friends or family, at least not that I’ve noticed. I might be a little more self-conscious around them sometimes, but I can’t say it’s really affected the relationships.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would stop worrying about your face if this one uneven-looking part were fixed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d stop completely. I think I’d feel better if that uneven part looked fixed, but right now it’s like I can’t stop staring at it in photos, especially since I’m in front of people more at work. Even when people say they barely see it, I still focus on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're mentally replaying or rechecking your appearance over and over in your head when you're not actually looking at yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like before I send event pictures or go into a vendor meeting, I’ll keep thinking about how my face looked, and then I end up checking it on my phone camera again and again. Sometimes I retake pictures a bunch of times or tilt my head a certain way to see if it looks less uneven.\n\nI’ve also asked a coworker if I look weird, which feels embarrassing. It can take up enough time that I fall behind on planning things.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or urges that you feel compelled to act on, unrelated to your appearance?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, not that I’ve noticed. The things I feel pushed to check or redo are mostly about how my face looks, not other stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your face because you're trying to make sure it looks perfect, or because you feel anxious if you don't check it?\n\nPatient: It’s more because I get anxious if I don’t check it. I don’t know if I’m trying to make it “perfect,” exactly… but before I send event pictures or go into a vendor meeting, I feel like I have to look at my face on my phone, retake the photo, tilt my head, that kind of thing. Sometimes I’ll even ask a coworker if I look weird. If I don’t do it, I keep worrying about it, and then I end up losing time and falling behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like something bad would happen if you didn't check your face before sending pictures or going into meetings?\n\nPatient: Not like something terrible would happen, exactly. It’s more that I get really anxious and stuck on it if I don’t check. Like before I send event pictures or go into a vendor meeting, I feel like I need to look at my face on my phone, maybe retake the photo or tilt my head to see if it looks less weird. Sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look okay. If I don’t do it, I keep thinking about it, and it can take up enough time that I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because, since I’ve had to be in front of people more for work, I’ve been really stuck on this one part of my face that looks uneven to me. Especially in photos, I can’t stop looking at it. Other people say they barely notice anything, but I keep seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Does this concern about your face affect other parts of your life beyond just being in front of people or looking at photos?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. It’s started affecting my work, not just photos. I actually missed part of a planning deadline because I spent so much time worrying about how I looked in the event materials. And now things like vendor calls and meeting prep take me way longer than they should, because I get stuck on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself performing any specific rituals or behaviors in an attempt to alleviate your concern about your face?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before I send event pictures or get on a vendor meeting, I’ll check my face in my phone camera over and over. Sometimes I retake pictures a bunch of times or tilt my head a certain way so it looks less uneven. I’ll also ask a coworker if I look weird. It’s embarrassing, but it can take up enough time that I fall behind on planning work.\n\nDoctor: Is the part of your face that you're concerned about something that you think is actually malformed or abnormal in some way?\n\nPatient: I mean, to me it does look abnormal, like one part of my face is uneven. But I know other people say they barely notice it, so I’m not sure if it’s actually malformed or if I’m just seeing it that way. I just can’t stop focusing on it, especially in photos.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern limited to this one part of your face that you mentioned looks uneven to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s mostly that one uneven-looking part of my face. Since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, I just keep noticing it in photos and staring at it, even though other people tell me they barely see anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would look perfect if this one part were fixed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say perfect. It’s more like… ever since I’ve had to be in front of people more at work, I can’t stop focusing on that one uneven-looking part in photos. Even when everyone else says they barely notice anything, it still really bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think it’s caused any major problems with my friends or family, at least not that I’ve noticed. I might be a little more self-conscious around them sometimes, but I can’t say it’s really affected the relationships.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would stop worrying about your face if this one uneven-looking part were fixed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d stop completely. I think I’d feel better if that uneven part looked fixed, but right now it’s like I can’t stop staring at it in photos, especially since I’m in front of people more at work. Even when people say they barely see it, I still focus on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're mentally replaying or rechecking your appearance over and over in your head when you're not actually looking at yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like before I send event pictures or go into a vendor meeting, I’ll keep thinking about how my face looked, and then I end up checking it on my phone camera again and again. Sometimes I retake pictures a bunch of times or tilt my head a certain way to see if it looks less uneven.\n\nI’ve also asked a coworker if I look weird, which feels embarrassing. It can take up enough time that I fall behind on planning things.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or urges that you feel compelled to act on, unrelated to your appearance?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, not that I’ve noticed. The things I feel pushed to check or redo are mostly about how my face looks, not other stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your face because you're trying to make sure it looks perfect, or because you feel anxious if you don't check it?\n\nPatient: It’s more because I get anxious if I don’t check it. I don’t know if I’m trying to make it “perfect,” exactly… but before I send event pictures or go into a vendor meeting, I feel like I have to look at my face on my phone, retake the photo, tilt my head, that kind of thing. Sometimes I’ll even ask a coworker if I look weird. If I don’t do it, I keep worrying about it, and then I end up losing time and falling behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like something bad would happen if you didn't check your face before sending pictures or going into meetings?\n\nPatient: Not like something terrible would happen, exactly. It’s more that I get really anxious and stuck on it if I don’t check. Like before I send event pictures or go into a vendor meeting, I feel like I need to look at my face on my phone, maybe retake the photo or tilt my head to see if it looks less weird. Sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look okay. If I don’t do it, I keep thinking about it, and it can take up enough time that I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face is a way to prevent something from going wrong?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. Not like I think something dangerous will happen, but I worry the picture will look bad or I’ll go into the vendor meeting looking weird. So I end up checking my face on my phone, retaking photos, tilting my head, or asking a coworker if I look okay. It can take long enough that I get behind on planning things.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Even when people say they barely see it, I still focus on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're mentally replaying or rechecking your appearance over and over in your head when you're not actually looking at yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like before I send event pictures or go into a vendor meeting, I’ll keep thinking about how my face looked, and then I end up checking it on my phone camera again and again. Sometimes I retake pictures a bunch of times or tilt my head a certain way to see if it looks less uneven.\n\nI’ve also asked a coworker if I look weird, which feels embarrassing. It can take up enough time that I fall behind on planning things.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or urges that you feel compelled to act on, unrelated to your appearance?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, not that I’ve noticed. The things I feel pushed to check or redo are mostly about how my face looks, not other stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your face because you're trying to make sure it looks perfect, or because you feel anxious if you don't check it?\n\nPatient: It’s more because I get anxious if I don’t check it. I don’t know if I’m trying to make it “perfect,” exactly… but before I send event pictures or go into a vendor meeting, I feel like I have to look at my face on my phone, retake the photo, tilt my head, that kind of thing. Sometimes I’ll even ask a coworker if I look weird. If I don’t do it, I keep worrying about it, and then I end up losing time and falling behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like something bad would happen if you didn't check your face before sending pictures or going into meetings?\n\nPatient: Not like something terrible would happen, exactly. It’s more that I get really anxious and stuck on it if I don’t check. Like before I send event pictures or go into a vendor meeting, I feel like I need to look at my face on my phone, maybe retake the photo or tilt my head to see if it looks less weird. Sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look okay. If I don’t do it, I keep thinking about it, and it can take up enough time that I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face is a way to prevent something from going wrong?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. Not like I think something dangerous will happen, but I worry the picture will look bad or I’ll go into the vendor meeting looking weird. So I end up checking my face on my phone, retaking photos, tilting my head, or asking a coworker if I look okay. It can take long enough that I get behind on planning things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would still need to check your face repeatedly even if you were certain that it looked fine?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I still would. Even if someone told me it looked fine, I’d probably still check my phone camera again or retake the picture, just to feel sure. Before vendor meetings or sending event photos, I get stuck doing that—tilting my head, checking angles, sometimes asking a coworker if I look weird. It can take up enough time that I fall behind on the planning work.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

And now things like vendor calls and meeting prep take me way longer than they should, because I get stuck on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself performing any specific rituals or behaviors in an attempt to alleviate your concern about your face?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before I send event pictures or get on a vendor meeting, I’ll check my face in my phone camera over and over. Sometimes I retake pictures a bunch of times or tilt my head a certain way so it looks less uneven. I’ll also ask a coworker if I look weird. It’s embarrassing, but it can take up enough time that I fall behind on planning work.\n\nDoctor: Is the part of your face that you're concerned about something that you think is actually malformed or abnormal in some way?\n\nPatient: I mean, to me it does look abnormal, like one part of my face is uneven. But I know other people say they barely notice it, so I’m not sure if it’s actually malformed or if I’m just seeing it that way. I just can’t stop focusing on it, especially in photos.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern limited to this one part of your face that you mentioned looks uneven to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s mostly that one uneven-looking part of my face. Since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, I just keep noticing it in photos and staring at it, even though other people tell me they barely see anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would look perfect if this one part were fixed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say perfect. It’s more like… ever since I’ve had to be in front of people more at work, I can’t stop focusing on that one uneven-looking part in photos. Even when everyone else says they barely notice anything, it still really bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think it’s caused any major problems with my friends or family, at least not that I’ve noticed. I might be a little more self-conscious around them sometimes, but I can’t say it’s really affected the relationships.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would stop worrying about your face if this one uneven-looking part were fixed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d stop completely. I think I’d feel better if that uneven part looked fixed, but right now it’s like I can’t stop staring at it in photos, especially since I’m in front of people more at work. Even when people say they barely see it, I still focus on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're mentally replaying or rechecking your appearance over and over in your head when you're not actually looking at yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like before I send event pictures or go into a vendor meeting, I’ll keep thinking about how my face looked, and then I end up checking it on my phone camera again and again. Sometimes I retake pictures a bunch of times or tilt my head a certain way to see if it looks less uneven.\n\nI’ve also asked a coworker if I look weird, which feels embarrassing. It can take up enough time that I fall behind on planning things.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or urges that you feel compelled to act on, unrelated to your appearance?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, not that I’ve noticed. The things I feel pushed to check or redo are mostly about how my face looks, not other stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your face because you're trying to make sure it looks perfect, or because you feel anxious if you don't check it?\n\nPatient: It’s more because I get anxious if I don’t check it. I don’t know if I’m trying to make it “perfect,” exactly… but before I send event pictures or go into a vendor meeting, I feel like I have to look at my face on my phone, retake the photo, tilt my head, that kind of thing. Sometimes I’ll even ask a coworker if I look weird. If I don’t do it, I keep worrying about it, and then I end up losing time and falling behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like something bad would happen if you didn't check your face before sending pictures or going into meetings?\n\nPatient: Not like something terrible would happen, exactly. It’s more that I get really anxious and stuck on it if I don’t check. Like before I send event pictures or go into a vendor meeting, I feel like I need to look at my face on my phone, maybe retake the photo or tilt my head to see if it looks less weird. Sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look okay. If I don’t do it, I keep thinking about it, and it can take up enough time that I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face is a way to prevent something from going wrong?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. Not like I think something dangerous will happen, but I worry the picture will look bad or I’ll go into the vendor meeting looking weird. So I end up checking my face on my phone, retaking photos, tilting my head, or asking a coworker if I look okay. It can take long enough that I get behind on planning things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would still need to check your face repeatedly even if you were certain that it looked fine?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I still would. Even if someone told me it looked fine, I’d probably still check my phone camera again or retake the picture, just to feel sure. Before vendor meetings or sending event photos, I get stuck doing that—tilting my head, checking angles, sometimes asking a coworker if I look weird. It can take up enough time that I fall behind on the planning work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face if you didn't think it was uneven?\n\nPatient: I think so, yeah. Even if I wasn’t focused on it being uneven, I’d probably still check my phone camera before sending event photos or joining a vendor meeting. I’d still retake pictures, tilt my head a certain way, or ask a coworker if I look weird. It just kind of eats up time, and then I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

If I don’t do it, I keep thinking about it, and it can take up enough time that I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face is a way to prevent something from going wrong?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. Not like I think something dangerous will happen, but I worry the picture will look bad or I’ll go into the vendor meeting looking weird. So I end up checking my face on my phone, retaking photos, tilting my head, or asking a coworker if I look okay. It can take long enough that I get behind on planning things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would still need to check your face repeatedly even if you were certain that it looked fine?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I still would. Even if someone told me it looked fine, I’d probably still check my phone camera again or retake the picture, just to feel sure. Before vendor meetings or sending event photos, I get stuck doing that—tilting my head, checking angles, sometimes asking a coworker if I look weird. It can take up enough time that I fall behind on the planning work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face if you didn't think it was uneven?\n\nPatient: I think so, yeah. Even if I wasn’t focused on it being uneven, I’d probably still check my phone camera before sending event photos or joining a vendor meeting. I’d still retake pictures, tilt my head a certain way, or ask a coworker if I look weird. It just kind of eats up time, and then I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face is something you do because of how it looks, or is it more like a habit that you feel you need to do?\n\nPatient: It’s more because of how it looks, I think. Like before I send event pictures or go into a vendor meeting, I start worrying my face looks weird, so I check it in my phone camera, retake photos, tilt my head a certain way, or ask a coworker if I look okay.   {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because, since I’ve had to be in front of people more for work, I’ve been really stuck on this one part of my face that looks uneven to me. Especially in photos, I can’t stop looking at it. Other people say they barely notice anything, but I keep seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Does this concern about your face affect other parts of your life beyond just being in front of people or looking at photos?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. It’s started affecting my work, not just photos. I actually missed part of a planning deadline because I spent so much time worrying about how I looked in the event materials. And now things like vendor calls and meeting prep take me way longer than they should, because I get stuck on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself performing any specific rituals or behaviors in an attempt to alleviate your concern about your face?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before I send event pictures or get on a vendor meeting, I’ll check my face in my phone camera over and over. Sometimes I retake pictures a bunch of times or tilt my head a certain way so it looks less uneven. I’ll also ask a coworker if I look weird. It’s embarrassing, but it can take up enough time that I fall behind on planning work.\n\nDoctor: Is the part of your face that you're concerned about something that you think is actually malformed or abnormal in some way?\n\nPatient: I mean, to me it does look abnormal, like one part of my face is uneven. But I know other people say they barely notice it, so I’m not sure if it’s actually malformed or if I’m just seeing it that way. I just can’t stop focusing on it, especially in photos.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern limited to this one part of your face that you mentioned looks uneven to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s mostly that one uneven-looking part of my face. Since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, I just keep noticing it in photos and staring at it, even though other people tell me they barely see anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would look perfect if this one part were fixed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say perfect. It’s more like… ever since I’ve had to be in front of people more at work, I can’t stop focusing on that one uneven-looking part in photos. Even when everyone else says they barely notice anything, it still really bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think it’s caused any major problems with my friends or family, at least not that I’ve noticed. I might be a little more self-conscious around them sometimes, but I can’t say it’s really affected the relationships.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would stop worrying about your face if this one uneven-looking part were fixed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d stop completely. I think I’d feel better if that uneven part looked fixed, but right now it’s like I can’t stop staring at it in photos, especially since I’m in front of people more at work. Even when people say they barely see it, I still focus on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're mentally replaying or rechecking your appearance over and over in your head when you're not actually looking at yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like before I send event pictures or go into a vendor meeting, I’ll keep thinking about how my face looked, and then I end up checking it on my phone camera again and again. Sometimes I retake pictures a bunch of times or tilt my head a certain way to see if it looks less uneven.\n\nI’ve also asked a coworker if I look weird, which feels embarrassing. It can take up enough time that I fall behind on planning things.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or urges that you feel compelled to act on, unrelated to your appearance?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, not that I’ve noticed. The things I feel pushed to check or redo are mostly about how my face looks, not other stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your face because you're trying to make sure it looks perfect, or because you feel anxious if you don't check it?\n\nPatient: It’s more because I get anxious if I don’t check it. I don’t know if I’m trying to make it “perfect,” exactly… but before I send event pictures or go into a vendor meeting, I feel like I have to look at my face on my phone, retake the photo, tilt my head, that kind of thing. Sometimes I’ll even ask a coworker if I look weird. If I don’t do it, I keep worrying about it, and then I end up losing time and falling behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like something bad would happen if you didn't check your face before sending pictures or going into meetings?\n\nPatient: Not like something terrible would happen, exactly. It’s more that I get really anxious and stuck on it if I don’t check. Like before I send event pictures or go into a vendor meeting, I feel like I need to look at my face on my phone, maybe retake the photo or tilt my head to see if it looks less weird. Sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look okay. If I don’t do it, I keep thinking about it, and it can take up enough time that I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face is a way to prevent something from going wrong?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. Not like I think something dangerous will happen, but I worry the picture will look bad or I’ll go into the vendor meeting looking weird. So I end up checking my face on my phone, retaking photos, tilting my head, or asking a coworker if I look okay. It can take long enough that I get behind on planning things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would still need to check your face repeatedly even if you were certain that it looked fine?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I still would. It can take up enough time that I fall behind on the planning work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face if you didn't think it was uneven?\n\nPatient: I think so, yeah. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because, since I’ve had to be in front of people more for work, I’ve been really stuck on this one part of my face that looks uneven to me. Especially in photos, I can’t stop looking at it. Other people say they barely notice anything, but I keep seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Does this concern about your face affect other parts of your life beyond just being in front of people or looking at photos?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. It’s started affecting my work, not just photos. I actually missed part of a planning deadline because I spent so much time worrying about how I looked in the event materials. And now things like vendor calls and meeting prep take me way longer than they should, because I get stuck on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself performing any specific rituals or behaviors in an attempt to alleviate your concern about your face?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before I send event pictures or get on a vendor meeting, I’ll check my face in my phone camera over and over. Sometimes I retake pictures a bunch of times or tilt my head a certain way so it looks less uneven. I’ll also ask a coworker if I look weird. It’s embarrassing, but it can take up enough time that I fall behind on planning work.\n\nDoctor: Is the part of your face that you're concerned about something that you think is actually malformed or abnormal in some way?\n\nPatient: I mean, to me it does look abnormal, like one part of my face is uneven. But I know other people say they barely notice it, so I’m not sure if it’s actually malformed or if I’m just seeing it that way. I just can’t stop focusing on it, especially in photos.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern limited to this one part of your face that you mentioned looks uneven to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s mostly that one uneven-looking part of my face. Since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, I just keep noticing it in photos and staring at it, even though other people tell me they barely see anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would look perfect if this one part were fixed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say perfect. It’s more like… ever since I’ve had to be in front of people more at work, I can’t stop focusing on that one uneven-looking part in photos. Even when everyone else says they barely notice anything, it still really bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think it’s caused any major problems with my friends or family, at least not that I’ve noticed. I might be a little more self-conscious around them sometimes, but I can’t say it’s really affected the relationships.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would stop worrying about your face if this one uneven-looking part were fixed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d stop completely. I think I’d feel better if that uneven part looked fixed, but right now it’s like I can’t stop staring at it in photos, especially since I’m in front of people more at work. Even when people say they barely see it, I still focus on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're mentally replaying or rechecking your appearance over and over in your head when you're not actually looking at yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like before I send event pictures or go into a vendor meeting, I’ll keep thinking about how my face looked, and then I end up checking it on my phone camera again and again. Sometimes I retake pictures a bunch of times or tilt my head a certain way to see if it looks less uneven.\n\nI’ve also asked a coworker if I look weird, which feels embarrassing. It can take up enough time that I fall behind on planning things.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or urges that you feel compelled to act on, unrelated to your appearance?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, not that I’ve noticed. The things I feel pushed to check or redo are mostly about how my face looks, not other stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your face because you're trying to make sure it looks perfect, or because you feel anxious if you don't check it?\n\nPatient: It’s more because I get anxious if I don’t check it. I don’t know if I’m trying to make it “perfect,” exactly… but before I send event pictures or go into a vendor meeting, I feel like I have to look at my face on my phone, retake the photo, tilt my head, that kind of thing. Sometimes I’ll even ask a coworker if I look weird. If I don’t do it, I keep worrying about it, and then I end up losing time and falling behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like something bad would happen if you didn't check your face before sending pictures or going into meetings?\n\nPatient: Not like something terrible would happen, exactly. It’s more that I get really anxious and stuck on it if I don’t check. Like before I send event pictures or go into a vendor meeting, I feel like I need to look at my face on my phone, maybe retake the photo or tilt my head to see if it looks less weird. Sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look okay. If I don’t do it, I keep thinking about it, and it can take up enough time that I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face is a way to prevent something from going wrong?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. Not like I think something dangerous will happen, but I worry the picture will look bad or I’ll go into the vendor meeting looking weird. So I end up checking my face on my phone, retaking photos, tilting my head, or asking a coworker if I look okay. It can take long enough that I get behind on planning things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would still need to check your face repeatedly even if you were certain that it looked fine?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I still would. Even if someone told me it looked fine, I’d probably still check my phone camera again or retake the picture, just to feel sure. Before vendor meetings or sending event photos, I get stuck doing that—tilting my head, checking angles, sometimes asking a coworker if I look weird. It can take up enough time that I fall behind on the planning work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face if you didn't think it was uneven?\n\nPatient: I think so, yeah. I’d still retake pictures, tilt my head a certain way, or ask a coworker if I look weird. It just kind of eats up time, and then I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face is something you do because of how it looks, or is it more like a habit that you feel you need to do?\n\nPatient: It’s more because of how it looks, I think. And yeah, it can take long enough that I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious if you didn't check your face, even if you weren't thinking about how it looks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it would happen if I really wasn’t thinking about how I look. Like before sending event pictures or going into a vendor meeting, I’ll check my phone camera, retake photos, tilt my head, or ask a coworker. If I didn’t do that, I’d feel really unsettled and distracted, and then I can lose time and fall behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to feel less anxious when you check your face, or are you trying to make your face look perfect?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more to feel less anxious, but also to make sure it looks okay. Like, before I send event pictures or join a vendor meeting, I’ll check my face on my phone, retake photos, tilt my head a certain way, or ask a coworker if I look weird. I’m trying to get it to where I can stop worrying about it, but it can take up enough time that I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because, since I’ve had to be in front of people more for work, I’ve been really stuck on this one part of my face that looks uneven to me. Especially in photos, I can’t stop looking at it. Other people say they barely notice anything, but I keep seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Does this concern about your face affect other parts of your life beyond just being in front of people or looking at photos?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. It’s started affecting my work, not just photos. I actually missed part of a planning deadline because I spent so much time worrying about how I looked in the event materials. And now things like vendor calls and meeting prep take me way longer than they should, because I get stuck on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself performing any specific rituals or behaviors in an attempt to alleviate your concern about your face?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before I send event pictures or get on a vendor meeting, I’ll check my face in my phone camera over and over. Sometimes I retake pictures a bunch of times or tilt my head a certain way so it looks less uneven. I’ll also ask a coworker if I look weird. It’s embarrassing, but it can take up enough time that I fall behind on planning work.\n\nDoctor: Is the part of your face that you're concerned about something that you think is actually malformed or abnormal in some way?\n\nPatient: I mean, to me it does look abnormal, like one part of my face is uneven. But I know other people say they barely notice it, so I’m not sure if it’s actually malformed or if I’m just seeing it that way. I just can’t stop focusing on it, especially in photos.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern limited to this one part of your face that you mentioned looks uneven to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s mostly that one uneven-looking part of my face. Since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, I just keep noticing it in photos and staring at it, even though other people tell me they barely see anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would look perfect if this one part were fixed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say perfect. It’s more like… ever since I’ve had to be in front of people more at work, I can’t stop focusing on that one uneven-looking part in photos. Even when everyone else says they barely notice anything, it still really bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think it’s caused any major problems with my friends or family, at least not that I’ve noticed. I might be a little more self-conscious around them sometimes, but I can’t say it’s really affected the relationships.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would stop worrying about your face if this one uneven-looking part were fixed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d stop completely. I think I’d feel better if that uneven part looked fixed, but right now it’s like I can’t stop staring at it in photos, especially since I’m in front of people more at work. Even when people say they barely see it, I still focus on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're mentally replaying or rechecking your appearance over and over in your head when you're not actually looking at yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like before I send event pictures or go into a vendor meeting, I’ll keep thinking about how my face looked, and then I end up checking it on my phone camera again and again. Sometimes I retake pictures a bunch of times or tilt my head a certain way to see if it looks less uneven.\n\nI’ve also asked a coworker if I look weird, which feels embarrassing. It can take up enough time that I fall behind on planning things.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or urges that you feel compelled to act on, unrelated to your appearance?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, not that I’ve noticed. The things I feel pushed to check or redo are mostly about how my face looks, not other stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your face because you're trying to make sure it looks perfect, or because you feel anxious if you don't check it?\n\nPatient: It’s more because I get anxious if I don’t check it. I don’t know if I’m trying to make it “perfect,” exactly… but before I send event pictures or go into a vendor meeting, I feel like I have to look at my face on my phone, retake the photo, tilt my head, that kind of thing. Sometimes I’ll even ask a coworker if I look weird. If I don’t do it, I keep worrying about it, and then I end up losing time and falling behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like something bad would happen if you didn't check your face before sending pictures or going into meetings?\n\nPatient: Not like something terrible would happen, exactly. It’s more that I get really anxious and stuck on it if I don’t check. Like before I send event pictures or go into a vendor meeting, I feel like I need to look at my face on my phone, maybe retake the photo or tilt my head to see if it looks less weird. Sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look okay. If I don’t do it, I keep thinking about it, and it can take up enough time that I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face is a way to prevent something from going wrong?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. Not like I think something dangerous will happen, but I worry the picture will look bad or I’ll go into the vendor meeting looking weird. So I end up checking my face on my phone, retaking photos, tilting my head, or asking a coworker if I look okay. It can take long enough that I get behind on planning things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would still need to check your face repeatedly even if you were certain that it looked fine?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I still would. Even if someone told me it looked fine, I’d probably still check my phone camera again or retake the picture, just to feel sure. Before vendor meetings or sending event photos, I get stuck doing that—tilting my head, checking angles, sometimes asking a coworker if I look weird. It can take up enough time that I fall behind on the planning work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face if you didn't think it was uneven?\n\nPatient: I think so, yeah. Even if I wasn’t focused on it being uneven, I’d probably still check my phone camera before sending event photos or joining a vendor meeting. I’d still retake pictures, tilt my head a certain way, or ask a coworker if I look weird. It just kind of eats up time, and then I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face is something you do because of how it looks, or is it more like a habit that you feel you need to do?\n\nPatient: It’s more because of how it looks, I think. Like before I send event pictures or go into a vendor meeting, I start worrying my face looks weird, so I check it in my phone camera, retake photos, tilt my head a certain way, or ask a coworker if I look okay. It feels like I need to do it, but it starts with how I think I look. And yeah, it can take long enough that I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious if you didn't check your face, even if you weren't thinking about how it looks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it would happen if I really wasn’t thinking about how I look. Like, before I send event pictures or join a vendor meeting, I’ll check my face on my phone, retake photos, tilt my head a certain way, or ask a coworker if I look weird. I’m trying to get it to where I can stop worrying about it, but it can take up enough time that I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your face needs to be symmetrical for you to feel comfortable with how you look?\n\nPatient: Yeah… kind of. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because, since I’ve had to be in front of people more for work, I’ve been really stuck on this one part of my face that looks uneven to me. Especially in photos, I can’t stop looking at it. Other people say they barely notice anything, but I keep seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Does this concern about your face affect other parts of your life beyond just being in front of people or looking at photos?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. It’s started affecting my work, not just photos. I actually missed part of a planning deadline because I spent so much time worrying about how I looked in the event materials. And now things like vendor calls and meeting prep take me way longer than they should, because I get stuck on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself performing any specific rituals or behaviors in an attempt to alleviate your concern about your face?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before I send event pictures or get on a vendor meeting, I’ll check my face in my phone camera over and over. Sometimes I retake pictures a bunch of times or tilt my head a certain way so it looks less uneven. I’ll also ask a coworker if I look weird. It’s embarrassing, but it can take up enough time that I fall behind on planning work.\n\nDoctor: Is the part of your face that you're concerned about something that you think is actually malformed or abnormal in some way?\n\nPatient: I mean, to me it does look abnormal, like one part of my face is uneven. But I know other people say they barely notice it, so I’m not sure if it’s actually malformed or if I’m just seeing it that way. I just can’t stop focusing on it, especially in photos.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern limited to this one part of your face that you mentioned looks uneven to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s mostly that one uneven-looking part of my face. Since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, I just keep noticing it in photos and staring at it, even though other people tell me they barely see anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would look perfect if this one part were fixed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say perfect. It’s more like… ever since I’ve had to be in front of people more at work, I can’t stop focusing on that one uneven-looking part in photos. Even when everyone else says they barely notice anything, it still really bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think it’s caused any major problems with my friends or family, at least not that I’ve noticed. I might be a little more self-conscious around them sometimes, but I can’t say it’s really affected the relationships.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would stop worrying about your face if this one uneven-looking part were fixed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d stop completely. I think I’d feel better if that uneven part looked fixed, but right now it’s like I can’t stop staring at it in photos, especially since I’m in front of people more at work. Even when people say they barely see it, I still focus on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're mentally replaying or rechecking your appearance over and over in your head when you're not actually looking at yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like before I send event pictures or go into a vendor meeting, I’ll keep thinking about how my face looked, and then I end up checking it on my phone camera again and again. Sometimes I retake pictures a bunch of times or tilt my head a certain way to see if it looks less uneven.\n\nI’ve also asked a coworker if I look weird, which feels embarrassing. It can take up enough time that I fall behind on planning things.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or urges that you feel compelled to act on, unrelated to your appearance?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, not that I’ve noticed. The things I feel pushed to check or redo are mostly about how my face looks, not other stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your face because you're trying to make sure it looks perfect, or because you feel anxious if you don't check it?\n\nPatient: It’s more because I get anxious if I don’t check it. I don’t know if I’m trying to make it “perfect,” exactly… but before I send event pictures or go into a vendor meeting, I feel like I have to look at my face on my phone, retake the photo, tilt my head, that kind of thing. Sometimes I’ll even ask a coworker if I look weird. If I don’t do it, I keep worrying about it, and then I end up losing time and falling behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like something bad would happen if you didn't check your face before sending pictures or going into meetings?\n\nPatient: Not like something terrible would happen, exactly. It’s more that I get really anxious and stuck on it if I don’t check. Like before I send event pictures or go into a vendor meeting, I feel like I need to look at my face on my phone, maybe retake the photo or tilt my head to see if it looks less weird. Sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look okay. If I don’t do it, I keep thinking about it, and it can take up enough time that I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face is a way to prevent something from going wrong?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. Not like I think something dangerous will happen, but I worry the picture will look bad or I’ll go into the vendor meeting looking weird. So I end up checking my face on my phone, retaking photos, tilting my head, or asking a coworker if I look okay. It can take long enough that I get behind on planning things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would still need to check your face repeatedly even if you were certain that it looked fine?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I still would. Even if someone told me it looked fine, I’d probably still check my phone camera again or retake the picture, just to feel sure. Before vendor meetings or sending event photos, I get stuck doing that—tilting my head, checking angles, sometimes asking a coworker if I look weird. It can take up enough time that I fall behind on the planning work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face if you didn't think it was uneven?\n\nPatient: I think so, yeah. Even if I wasn’t focused on it being uneven, I’d probably still check my phone camera before sending event photos or joining a vendor meeting. I’d still retake pictures, tilt my head a certain way, or ask a coworker if I look weird. It just kind of eats up time, and then I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face is something you do because of how it looks, or is it more like a habit that you feel you need to do?\n\nPatient: It’s more because of how it looks, I think. Like before I send event pictures or go into a vendor meeting, I start worrying my face looks weird, so I check it in my phone camera, retake photos, tilt my head a certain way, or ask a coworker if I look okay. It feels like I need to do it, but it starts with how I think I look. And yeah, it can take long enough that I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious if you didn't check your face, even if you weren't thinking about how it looks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it would happen if I really wasn’t thinking about how I look. Other people tell me they barely see anything, but I still feel uncomfortable if it looks uneven.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face if a doctor told you that there was nothing medically wrong with your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I still would. Even if a doctor said nothing was medically wrong, I’d probably still check before sending event pictures or getting on a vendor meeting. I’ll look at my face in my phone camera, retake pictures, tilt my head a certain way, or ask a coworker if I look weird. It’s like I need to make sure for myself, and it can take long enough that I fall behind on planning work."
  }
